<a id='question-059'></a>

### Question 059: Gastroduodenal Artery: Runs Posterior to 1st Part of Duodenum (Bleeding in Posterior Duodenal Ulcer)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The anatomical relationship between the gastroduodenal artery (GDA) and the first part of the duodenum, specifically that the GDA runs *posterior* to the first part of the duodenum, making it vulnerable to erosion from posterior duodenal ulcers. (胃十二指肠动脉与十二指肠第一部分之间的解剖关系，特别是胃十二指肠动脉位于十二指肠第一部分*后方*，使其容易受到十二指肠后壁溃疡的侵蚀。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic NSAID use presents to the emergency department complaining of sudden onset, painless melena and dizziness. His vital signs show a heart rate of 115 bpm, blood pressure of 95/60 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 22 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals pallor and epigastric tenderness. Laboratory results show a hemoglobin level of 7.5 g/dL. An emergent upper endoscopy reveals a large, deep ulcer located on the posterior wall of the first part of the duodenum. During the procedure, the endoscopist observes active arterial bleeding from the base of the ulcer.

Which of the following arteries is most likely the source of the bleeding?

(A) Right Gastric Artery
(B) Left Gastric Artery
(C) Superior Pancreaticoduodenal Artery
(D) Common Hepatic Artery
(E) Gastroduodenal Artery

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male, chronic NSAID use (risk factor for peptic ulcers).
- **Symptoms:** Sudden onset, painless melena (suggests upper GI bleed), dizziness (hypovolemia/anemia).
- **Vitals:** Tachycardia, hypotension (signs of hemorrhagic shock).
- **Labs:** Low hemoglobin (confirms significant blood loss).
- **Endoscopy Findings:** Large, deep ulcer on the *posterior* wall of the *first part of the duodenum*, active *arterial* bleeding.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the artery most likely responsible for the bleeding given the ulcer location.

The key clue is the location of the ulcer: *posterior* wall of the *first part of the duodenum*. This anatomical location is directly adjacent to the gastroduodenal artery, which runs posterior to this segment of the duodenum. Erosion of a posterior duodenal ulcer into this artery is a common cause of massive upper GI bleeding.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Gastroduodenal Artery (GDA):** Arises from the common hepatic artery (a branch of the celiac trunk). It descends posterior to the first part of the duodenum, where it typically divides into the superior pancreaticoduodenal artery (supplying the head of the pancreas and the first part of the duodenum) and the right gastroepiploic artery (supplying the greater curvature of the stomach).
- **First Part of Duodenum:** The initial segment of the duodenum, C-shaped, curving around the head of the pancreas. Its posterior wall is directly adjacent to the GDA.
- **Posterior Duodenal Ulcers:** Ulcers located on the posterior wall of the first part of the duodenum are particularly dangerous because they can erode into the GDA, leading to significant hemorrhage.
- **Other Arteries:**
    - **Right Gastric Artery:** Usually arises from the proper hepatic artery or the GDA itself. It supplies the lesser curvature of the stomach. It is not directly posterior to the first part of the duodenum.
    - **Left Gastric Artery:** Arises directly from the celiac trunk. It supplies the lesser curvature of the stomach and the lower esophagus. It is located more superiorly and anteriorly, not posterior to the duodenum.
    - **Superior Pancreaticoduodenal Artery:** A branch of the GDA. It runs *anterior* to the uncinate process of the pancreas and supplies the duodenum and pancreas. While related, the GDA itself is the primary vessel at risk from posterior duodenal erosion.
    - **Common Hepatic Artery:** Gives rise to the GDA and the proper hepatic artery. It is located superior to the GDA and duodenum.

Therefore, the anatomical relationship makes the GDA the most likely source of bleeding from a posterior duodenal ulcer.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Right Gastric Artery:** Incorrect. This artery supplies the stomach (lesser curvature) and arises from the proper hepatic artery or GDA. It is not located directly posterior to the first part of the duodenum. Erosion of a posterior duodenal ulcer would not typically involve this artery.
- (B) **Left Gastric Artery:** Incorrect. This artery arises from the celiac trunk and supplies the stomach (lesser curvature) and lower esophagus. It is located superiorly and anteriorly relative to the first part of the duodenum and is not at risk from posterior duodenal ulcers.
- (C) **Superior Pancreaticoduodenal Artery:** Incorrect. This is a *branch* of the GDA. While it supplies the duodenum, the GDA itself is the larger vessel running directly posterior to the first part of the duodenum and is the primary vessel at risk from erosion. Bleeding from the GDA itself is more likely to be massive than bleeding from its branch in this scenario.
- (D) **Common Hepatic Artery:** Incorrect. This artery is the parent vessel of the GDA. It is located superior to the duodenum and GDA. While damage to the common hepatic artery is possible, it's less likely than damage to the GDA from a posterior duodenal ulcer, which is in direct contact with the GDA.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location Relative to 1st Part of Duodenum | Relationship to Posterior Duodenal Ulcer | Clinical Relevance                                  |
| :---------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- |
| Gastroduodenal Artery (GDA)   | Posterior                                | Directly posterior, at risk of erosion   | Common source of massive bleeding from posterior ulcers |
| Right Gastric Artery          | Anterior/Superior (supplies stomach)     | Not directly related                     | Less likely source of bleeding from duodenal ulcer   |
| Left Gastric Artery           | Superior/Anterior (supplies stomach)     | Not directly related                     | Not related to duodenal ulcers                       |
| Superior Pancreaticoduodenal Artery | Anterior (branch of GDA)                | Not directly posterior                  | Branch of GDA, less likely primary source          |
| Common Hepatic Artery         | Superior                                | Not directly posterior                  | Parent vessel of GDA, less likely direct erosion   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The gastroduodenal artery runs posterior to the first part of the duodenum. Posterior duodenal ulcers can erode into this artery, causing significant hemorrhage. Differentiating the GDA from its branches (like the superior pancreaticoduodenal artery) and other vessels supplying the stomach (like the gastric arteries) is crucial for understanding the source of bleeding in this clinical scenario. Remember the "posterior" location of the GDA relative to the duodenum.

---

<a id='question-060'></a>

### Question 060: Left Gastric Artery: Runs along Lesser Curvature of Stomach (Bleeding in Gastric Ulcer)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the arterial supply to the lesser curvature of the stomach and its relationship to the vagus nerve, specifically the left gastric artery's course and potential involvement in bleeding gastric ulcers. (识别胃小弯的动脉供应及其与迷走神经的关系，特别是左胃动脉的走行及其在出血性胃溃疡中的潜在受累情况。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe epigastric pain, described as burning, and hematemesis (vomiting blood). He has a history of chronic NSAID use for osteoarthritis. Physical examination reveals tachycardia (110 bpm), hypotension (90/60 mmHg), and epigastric tenderness. An upper endoscopy reveals a large, actively bleeding ulcer located on the lesser curvature of the stomach, approximately 5 cm distal to the gastroesophageal junction. The endoscopist notes significant bleeding originating from a vessel adjacent to the ulcer crater. During attempted endoscopic hemostasis, the bleeding vessel is inadvertently injured, leading to further hemorrhage. The patient is immediately taken to the operating room for surgical intervention. Intraoperatively, the surgeon identifies the source of bleeding as a branch of an artery running along the lesser curvature of the stomach, closely associated with the anterior vagal trunk.

Which of the following arteries is most likely the source of the patient's bleeding?

(A) Right Gastric Artery
(B) Left Gastric Artery
(C) Short Gastric Artery
(D) Right Gastroepiploic Artery
(E) Left Gastroepiploic Artery

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 58-year-old male with epigastric pain, hematemesis, tachycardia, hypotension, history of NSAID use. This points towards a peptic ulcer with significant bleeding.
- **Endoscopy Findings:** Large, actively bleeding ulcer on the lesser curvature of the stomach, 5 cm distal to the GE junction. This localizes the ulcer and bleeding source.
- **Surgical Findings:** Bleeding vessel identified along the lesser curvature, closely associated with the anterior vagal trunk. This is the crucial anatomical clue.
- **Question Stem:** Asks to identify the artery supplying the lesser curvature in this region, given its association with the vagus nerve.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Lesser Curvature Blood Supply:** The lesser curvature of the stomach is primarily supplied by the left gastric artery (a direct branch of the celiac trunk) and the right gastric artery (typically a branch of the hepatic artery proper or gastroduodenal artery).
- **Left Gastric Artery:** Originates from the celiac trunk. It runs superiorly along the lesser curvature of the stomach towards the gastroesophageal junction. It gives off esophageal branches and anastomoses with the right gastric artery. Crucially, it is accompanied by the anterior vagal trunk (part of the vagus nerve) along its course.
- **Right Gastric Artery:** Originates from the hepatic artery proper or gastroduodenal artery. It runs inferiorly along the lesser curvature towards the pylorus, anastomosing with the left gastric artery.
- **Short Gastric Arteries:** Branches of the splenic artery, supplying the fundus of the stomach. They do not run along the lesser curvature.
- **Gastroepiploic Arteries (Right and Left):** Branches of the gastroduodenal artery (right) and splenic artery (left). They run along the greater curvature of the stomach, not the lesser curvature.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Bleeding ulcers on the lesser curvature are often associated with the left gastric artery or the right gastric artery. The description of the artery being "closely associated with the anterior vagal trunk" strongly points to the left gastric artery, as this nerve runs alongside it along the lesser curvature. NSAID use is a common cause of gastric ulcers.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Right Gastric Artery:** Supplies the lesser curvature, but typically runs more inferiorly towards the pylorus. While it can be involved in lesser curvature ulcers, it is not typically described as being closely associated with the anterior vagal trunk along the *entire* course relevant to an ulcer 5 cm distal to the GE junction. The left gastric artery's association with the vagus nerve is a more specific landmark.
- (C) **Short Gastric Artery:** Supplies the fundus (superior part) of the stomach. Ulcers in the fundus might bleed from these arteries, but the ulcer is located on the lesser curvature, making this option incorrect.
- (D) **Right Gastroepiploic Artery:** Supplies the greater curvature. Ulcers on the greater curvature would bleed from this artery or the left gastroepiploic artery. The ulcer is on the lesser curvature.
- (E) **Left Gastroepiploic Artery:** Supplies the greater curvature. Similar to the right gastroepiploic artery, this is incorrect because the ulcer is located on the lesser curvature.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Left Gastric Artery (Correct) | Right Gastric Artery | Short Gastric Arteries | Right Gastroepiploic Artery | Left Gastroepiploic Artery |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------|------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Origin**           | Celiac Trunk                    | Hepatic/Gastroduodenal | Splenic Artery         | Gastroduodenal Artery      | Splenic Artery         |
| **Course**           | Along Lesser Curvature (Superior) | Along Lesser Curvature (Inferior) | To Fundus              | Along Greater Curvature    | Along Greater Curvature    |
| **Anastomosis**      | Right Gastric Artery            | Left Gastric Artery   | None significant       | Left Gastroepiploic Artery | Right Gastroepiploic Artery |
| **Associated Nerve** | Anterior Vagal Trunk            | None specific         | None specific          | None specific               | None specific               |
| **Ulcer Location**   | Lesser Curvature (Proximal)     | Lesser Curvature (Distal) | Fundus                 | Greater Curvature           | Greater Curvature           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The left gastric artery is the primary blood supply to the proximal lesser curvature of the stomach and runs alongside the anterior vagal trunk. Bleeding ulcers in this region are frequently associated with this artery. Differentiating the arteries supplying the lesser curvature (left and right gastric) from those supplying the greater curvature (gastroepiploic) and the fundus (short gastric) is crucial for understanding the source of bleeding in peptic ulcer disease. Remember the anatomical relationships, especially the left gastric artery's association with the vagus nerve.

---

<a id='question-061'></a>

### Question 061: Splenic Artery: Tortuous Course along Superior Border of Pancreas (Splenic Artery Aneurysm & Pancreatitis Erosion)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The splenic artery's tortuous course along the superior border of the pancreatic body and tail, its relationship to the pancreatic duct, and the potential for complications like aneurysm formation or erosion by pancreatitis. (脾动脉沿着胰腺体和尾部上缘的弯曲走行，与胰管的关系，以及动脉瘤形成或胰腺炎侵蚀等并发症的可能性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic pancreatitis presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, constant epigastric pain radiating to his back, which began acutely 3 days ago. He reports nausea and vomiting. His past medical history includes heavy alcohol use. Physical examination reveals significant epigastric tenderness to palpation, guarding, and rebound tenderness. Laboratory results show elevated serum amylase and lipase levels, consistent with acute pancreatitis. A CT scan of the abdomen reveals an enlarged, tortuous splenic artery with a focal outpouching measuring 3 cm in diameter along its course superior to the pancreatic body. The outpouching appears to be eroding into the adjacent pancreatic parenchyma. The splenic vein is also dilated. Which of the following structures is most likely involved in the erosion caused by the splenic artery aneurysm in this patient?

(A) The left renal vein
(B) The superior mesenteric artery
(C) The main pancreatic duct (Duct of Wirsung)
(D) The common bile duct
(E) The portal vein

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with chronic pancreatitis and heavy alcohol use (risk factor for pancreatitis and vascular complications).
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Acute severe epigastric pain radiating to the back, nausea, vomiting (classic symptoms of acute pancreatitis).
- **Physical Exam:** Epigastric tenderness, guarding, rebound tenderness (signs of peritonitis, potentially related to pancreatitis or vascular complication).
- **Labs:** Elevated amylase and lipase (confirms acute pancreatitis).
- **CT Findings:** Enlarged, tortuous splenic artery with a 3 cm aneurysm along its course superior to the pancreatic body. Erosion into adjacent pancreatic parenchyma. Dilated splenic vein (portal hypertension secondary to chronic pancreatitis or splenic vein thrombosis).
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is most likely involved in the erosion caused by the splenic artery aneurysm.

The key anatomical relationship is the splenic artery's course along the superior border of the pancreatic body and tail. Aneurysms of the splenic artery, particularly in the setting of chronic pancreatitis, can erode into adjacent structures. The most intimately related structure running along the superior border of the pancreas, within the pancreatic groove, is the main pancreatic duct (Duct of Wirsung).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Splenic Artery Course:** Originates from the celiac trunk, runs posterior to the stomach, along the superior border of the pancreas (body and tail), reaching the splenic hilum. Its course is often tortuous, especially in chronic pancreatitis. (Kaplan Anatomy, p. 168; First Aid 2025, p. 170)
- **Pancreatic Duct (Duct of Wirsung):** Runs the length of the pancreas, typically within the groove between the stomach and the duodenum (pancreatic groove), along the superior border of the pancreatic body and tail, joining the common bile duct to form the ampulla of Vater before entering the duodenum. (Kaplan Anatomy, p. 172; First Aid 2025, p. 171)
- **Relationship:** The splenic artery and the main pancreatic duct lie in close proximity along the superior border of the pancreas. Chronic inflammation (pancreatitis) can weaken the arterial wall, leading to aneurysm formation. The aneurysm, or the inflammatory process itself, can then erode into the adjacent pancreatic duct.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Splenic artery aneurysms are the most common visceral artery aneurysms. They are frequently associated with chronic pancreatitis (up to 20% of patients). Complications include rupture (causing hemorrhage), thrombosis, distal embolization, and erosion into adjacent structures like the pancreatic duct or stomach. Erosion into the pancreatic duct can cause ductal stricture, obstruction, or pancreatitis.

##### 3. Embryological Origin:**
- **Splenic Artery:** Develops from the dorsal mesentery of the foregut.
- **Pancreatic Duct:** Develops from the dorsal pancreatic bud (forms the body and tail) and the ventral pancreatic bud (forms the head and uncinate process). The main pancreatic duct is primarily derived from the dorsal bud duct.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) The left renal vein: The left renal vein passes posterior to the splenic artery and superior mesenteric artery as they cross the aorta. While close, it is not directly adjacent to the splenic artery along the superior border of the pancreas. Erosion into the left renal vein is not a typical complication of splenic artery aneurysms related to pancreatitis in this location.
- (B) The superior mesenteric artery: The superior mesenteric artery (SMA) originates from the aorta inferior to the celiac trunk. It runs inferiorly, anterior to the uncinate process of the pancreas. It is not directly adjacent to the splenic artery along the superior border of the pancreatic body/tail where the aneurysm is described.
- (D) The common bile duct: The common bile duct runs posterior to the first part of the duodenum and then descends between the duodenum and the head of the pancreas, joining the pancreatic duct. It is located more medially and inferiorly compared to the splenic artery's course along the superior border of the body/tail. While they converge at the ampulla, direct erosion from a splenic artery aneurysm along the body/tail into the CBD is unlikely.
- (E) The portal vein: The portal vein is formed posterior to the neck of the pancreas by the confluence of the superior mesenteric vein and the splenic vein. While the splenic vein runs alongside the splenic artery, the portal vein itself is formed more medially and posteriorly. Erosion into the portal vein is less likely than erosion into the adjacent pancreatic duct.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                  | Location relative to Splenic Artery (along pancreatic body/tail) | Relationship to Pancreatic Duct | Likelihood of Erosion from Splenic Artery Aneurysm |
| :------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------ |
| Main Pancreatic Duct       | Runs in the groove superior to the pancreas, adjacent to artery | Intimate, runs alongside        | **High**                                          |
| Left Renal Vein            | Posterior to splenic artery near aorta crossing                 | Distant                         | Low                                               |
| Superior Mesenteric Artery | Inferior to splenic artery, anterior to uncinate process        | Distant                         | Low                                               |
| Common Bile Duct           | Runs posterior to duodenum, medial/inferior to pancreatic head   | Distant (converge at ampulla)   | Low                                               |
| Portal Vein                | Formed posterior to pancreatic neck (SMV + Splenic Vein)        | Medial/Posterior                | Low                                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The splenic artery's tortuous course along the superior border of the pancreatic body and tail places it in close proximity to the main pancreatic duct (Duct of Wirsung). Chronic pancreatitis can lead to splenic artery aneurysms, which can erode into adjacent structures, most commonly the pancreatic duct. Remember the anatomical relationships along the superior border of the pancreas. Key differentials for epigastric pain include pancreatitis, peptic ulcer disease, cholecystitis, and myocardial infarction. Key differentials for splenic artery aneurysm complications include rupture (hemorrhage) and erosion into adjacent structures.

<a id='question-062'></a>

### Question 062: Splenic Artery Ligation and Gastric Ischemia
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The short gastric arteries, branches of the splenic artery, supply the fundus and upper body of the stomach. They lack significant anastomoses with other gastric vessels, making the gastric fundus particularly vulnerable to ischemia if the splenic artery is ligated or occluded. (短胃动脉是脾动脉的分支，供应胃底和上部胃体。它们与其他胃血管之间缺乏显著的吻合，因此如果脾动脉被结扎或闭塞，胃底特别容易发生缺血。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department following a motor vehicle accident. He has sustained blunt abdominal trauma, resulting in a splenic rupture and hemoperitoneum. During emergency splenectomy, the surgeon meticulously ligates the splenic artery and its branches to control bleeding. Postoperatively, the patient develops severe epigastric pain, nausea, vomiting, and signs of peritonitis. An upper endoscopy reveals extensive mucosal necrosis and ulceration confined primarily to the fundus and proximal body of the stomach, sparing the antrum and pylorus. Which of the following arterial features best explains this specific pattern of gastric ischemia?

(A) The left gastric artery typically anastomoses extensively with the short gastric arteries, providing collateral circulation to the fundus.
(B) The right gastric artery, arising from the hepatic artery proper, provides significant collateral supply to the gastric fundus via the gastroepiploic trunk.
(C) The short gastric arteries, arising from the splenic artery, supply the gastric fundus and lack substantial anastomoses with other gastric vessels.
(D) The gastroepiploic arteries, branches of the gastroduodenal artery, primarily supply the greater curvature and anastomose freely with the short gastric arteries at the cardia.
(E) The superior mesenteric artery (SMA) provides direct branches to the gastric fundus via the pancreaticoduodenal arteries, ensuring adequate collateral flow.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62M, blunt abdominal trauma, splenic rupture, hemoperitoneum.
- **Surgical Procedure:** Emergency splenectomy with ligation of splenic artery and its branches.
- **Postoperative Complication:** Severe epigastric pain, nausea, vomiting, peritonitis.
- **Endoscopic Findings:** Extensive mucosal necrosis and ulceration limited to the gastric fundus and proximal body, sparing the antrum/pylorus.
- **Key Clue:** The specific location of the ischemia (fundus/proximal body) following ligation of the splenic artery and its branches points to the vascular supply of that region. The splenic artery supplies the fundus via the short gastric arteries.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Splenic Artery:** Arises from the celiac trunk. Runs along the superior border of the pancreas, posterior to the stomach.
- **Branches of Splenic Artery:** Gives rise to pancreatic branches, short gastric arteries (typically 4-6), and left gastroepiploic artery.
- **Short Gastric Arteries:** Arise from the splenic artery near the hilum of the spleen. They course superiorly and anteriorly, piercing the gastrosplenic ligament to reach the greater curvature of the stomach. They supply the fundus and upper part of the greater curvature.
- **Anastomoses:** The short gastric arteries have *limited* anastomoses with other gastric vessels. The primary blood supply to the fundus is via these vessels from the splenic artery. The left gastric artery (from the celiac trunk) supplies the lesser curvature and anastomoses with the left gastroepiploic artery (also from the splenic artery) along the greater curvature, but its contribution to the fundus is minimal compared to the short gastrics. The right gastric artery (from the hepatic artery proper) supplies the pylorus and anastomoses with the right gastroepiploic artery (from the gastroduodenal artery).
- **Clinical Correlation:** Ligation of the splenic artery during splenectomy cuts off the primary blood supply to the gastric fundus via the short gastric arteries. Due to the lack of significant collateral circulation from other gastric arteries, this leads to ischemia and necrosis of the fundus, as observed in the patient's endoscopy. This is a known complication of splenectomy, especially if the short gastric arteries are ligated along with the main splenic artery.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. The left gastric artery primarily supplies the lesser curvature and anastomoses with the left gastroepiploic artery along the greater curvature. It does *not* extensively anastomose with the short gastric arteries to provide significant collateral flow to the fundus. If it did, fundal ischemia after splenic artery ligation would be less likely.
- (B): Incorrect. The right gastric artery supplies the pylorus and anastomoses with the right gastroepiploic artery. It is anatomically distant from the fundus and does not provide significant collateral supply to this region.
- (D): Incorrect. The gastroepiploic arteries (left from splenic, right from gastroduodenal) primarily supply the greater curvature. While they anastomose with each other and with the short gastric arteries along the greater curvature, these anastomoses are not sufficient to compensate for the loss of blood supply from the short gastric arteries to the fundus. The primary supply to the fundus remains the short gastrics.
- (E): Incorrect. The SMA supplies the distal stomach (antrum, pylorus) and intestines via the gastroduodenal artery and its branches (right gastroepiploic, superior mesenteric arteries to the duodenum). It does not directly supply the fundus, and its branches do not provide adequate collateral circulation to compensate for the loss of splenic artery flow to the fundus.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Short Gastric Arteries (from Splenic Artery) | Left Gastric Artery (from Celiac Trunk) | Right Gastric Artery (from Hepatic Artery) | Left Gastroepiploic Artery (from Splenic Artery) | Right Gastroepiploic Artery (from Gastroduodenal Artery) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|
| **Origin**           | Splenic Artery                              | Celiac Trunk                             | Hepatic Artery Proper                      | Splenic Artery                                  | Gastroduodenal Artery                               |
| **Supply Area**      | Gastric Fundus, Upper Greater Curvature     | Lesser Curvature, Cardia                  | Pylorus                                   | Greater Curvature (distal to short gastrics)     | Greater Curvature (proximal to left gastroepiploic) |
| **Anastomoses**      | Limited with other gastric vessels          | With Left Gastroepiploic                  | With Right Gastroepiploic                  | With Left Gastric, Short Gastric                 | With Right Gastric, Left Gastroepiploic             |
| **Clinical Relevance**| Vulnerable to ischemia post-splenectomy    | Less relevant to fundal ischemia          | Less relevant to fundal ischemia          | Anastomoses help maintain greater curvature supply| Anastomoses help maintain greater curvature supply  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The short gastric arteries, branches of the splenic artery, are the primary blood supply to the gastric fundus. Due to their limited anastomoses with other gastric vessels, ligation of the splenic artery during splenectomy can lead to fundal ischemia. Remember the specific vascular territories of the stomach and the consequences of disrupting major arterial supply. Key differentials include understanding the supply territories of the left gastric, right gastric, and gastroepiploic arteries.

<a id='question-063'></a>

### Question 063: Superior Mesenteric Artery (SMA) Origin at L1: Crossed by Splenic Vein and Neck of Pancreas
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The origin of the Superior Mesenteric Artery (SMA) from the abdominal aorta at the level of the L1 vertebra and its relationship to the splenic vein and the neck of the pancreas. (SMA 起源于L1椎体水平的腹主动脉，与脾静脉和胰头/颈部关系)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic pancreatitis and heavy alcohol use undergoes a Whipple procedure (pancreaticoduodenectomy) for a locally advanced tumor involving the head of the pancreas. During the dissection, the surgeon identifies the superior mesenteric artery (SMA) arising from the abdominal aorta. As the surgeon proceeds with the pancreatic neck resection, they encounter significant bleeding from a vessel located posterior to the SMA and superior to the uncinate process of the pancreas. The vessel is identified as the splenic vein, which is intimately associated with the SMA at its origin. The surgeon carefully dissects the splenic vein away from the SMA to avoid injury during the procedure. Postoperatively, the patient develops signs of portal hypertension, including ascites and esophageal varices. Imaging reveals thrombosis of the splenic vein, likely due to surgical manipulation near the SMA.

Which of the following anatomical structures is most consistently located posterior to the origin of the Superior Mesenteric Artery (SMA) from the abdominal aorta?

(A) Inferior Mesenteric Artery (IMA)
(B) Left Renal Vein
(C) Splenic Vein
(D) Celiac Trunk
(E) Left Gonadal Artery

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male, chronic pancreatitis, heavy alcohol use. This history predisposes to pancreatic pathology and potential vascular complications.
- **Procedure:** Whipple procedure (pancreaticoduodenectomy). This surgery involves extensive dissection around the head and neck of the pancreas, duodenum, and biliary tree.
- **Anatomical Location:** The surgeon is dissecting near the origin of the SMA from the abdominal aorta.
- **Key Finding:** Bleeding from a vessel posterior to the SMA and superior to the uncinate process. This vessel is identified as the splenic vein.
- **Complication:** Postoperative thrombosis of the splenic vein, attributed to surgical manipulation near the SMA.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical structure consistently located posterior to the origin of the SMA.
- **Clue Analysis:** The vignette explicitly describes the splenic vein's location posterior to the SMA origin during a Whipple procedure. The complication of splenic vein thrombosis further emphasizes this close anatomical relationship. The question asks for the structure *most consistently* located posterior to the SMA origin.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **SMA Origin:** The SMA typically arises from the anterior surface of the abdominal aorta at the level of the L1 vertebra, just inferior to the celiac trunk (which arises at T12/L1).
- **SMA Course:** It initially courses anteriorly and slightly to the left, passing posterior to the celiac trunk and the median arcuate ligament.
- **Splenic Vein Course:** The splenic vein is formed by the union of the superior mesenteric vein and the short gastric veins posterior to the pancreas. It courses posteriorly, superior to the body and tail of the pancreas, and then runs posterior to the SMA and the abdominal aorta, joining the superior mesenteric vein anterior to the inferior mesenteric artery (IMA) to form the portal vein.
- **Anatomical Relationship:** The splenic vein consistently runs posterior to the SMA at its origin from the aorta. This relationship is crucial during abdominal surgery, particularly procedures involving the pancreas and duodenum (like the Whipple procedure), as the splenic vein is vulnerable to injury during dissection around the SMA.
- **Clinical Significance:** Injury or compression of the splenic vein can lead to thrombosis, resulting in portal hypertension, splenomegaly, and gastric/esophageal varices, as seen in the postoperative complication described in the vignette.
- **Embryology:** The SMA and splenic vein develop from different embryonic sources. The SMA arises from the dorsal mesentery, while the splenic vein develops from the dorsal mesogastrium. Their close relationship in the adult is a result of complex folding and rotation during development.
- **Kaplan Anatomy & First Aid:** Both resources emphasize the consistent posterior relationship of the splenic vein to the SMA origin. Kaplan diagrams clearly show the splenic vein posterior to the SMA. First Aid notes the close proximity and potential for injury during pancreatic surgery.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Inferior Mesenteric Artery (IMA): The IMA arises from the distal SMA, typically at the level of the L3 vertebra. It courses inferiorly to supply the distal colon. It is not located posterior to the SMA origin.
- (B) Left Renal Vein: The left renal vein passes anterior to the aorta and posterior to the SMA. It drains into the inferior vena cava. While it is in the vicinity, it is not consistently posterior to the SMA origin itself; rather, the SMA crosses anterior to the left renal vein.
- (D) Celiac Trunk: The celiac trunk arises from the abdominal aorta at the level of T12/L1, superior to the SMA origin. It courses superiorly and slightly to the left. It is not posterior to the SMA origin.
- (E) Left Gonadal Artery: The left gonadal artery (testicular or ovarian artery) typically arises directly from the abdominal aorta, usually just below the origin of the SMA or sometimes from the SMA itself. It courses inferiorly and laterally. It is not consistently posterior to the SMA origin.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location relative to SMA Origin (L1) | Key Relationship                                  | Clinical Relevance                                  |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|
| **Splenic Vein (C)** | Posterior                            | Consistently posterior to SMA origin; joins SMV to form portal vein | Vulnerable during pancreatic/splenic surgery; thrombosis causes portal hypertension |
| IMA (A)              | Distal SMA branch                    | Arises from SMA distally; supplies distal colon     | Mesenteric ischemia if compromised                 |
| Left Renal Vein (B)  | Anterior to SMA                      | SMA crosses anterior to LRV; drains left kidney    | Compression by SMA can cause renal vein thrombosis |
| Celiac Trunk (D)     | Superior                            | Arises superior to SMA; supplies foregut organs    | Occlusion affects foregut blood supply             |
| Left Gonadal Artery (E)| Inferior/Lateral (variable origin)   | Arises from aorta or SMA; supplies gonad          | Variable origin; potential for collateral circulation |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The splenic vein consistently runs posterior to the origin of the SMA from the abdominal aorta at L1. This anatomical relationship is critical knowledge for surgeons performing procedures in the retroperitoneum, especially around the pancreas, as the splenic vein is susceptible to injury during dissection near the SMA. Remember the mnemonic "SMA crosses anterior to the Left Renal Vein" and the "Splenic Vein is posterior to the SMA". Key differentials include the IMA (distal branch), Left Renal Vein (anterior), Celiac Trunk (superior), and Left Gonadal Artery (variable origin).

<a id='question-064'></a>

### Question 064: SMA Branches: Inferior Pancreaticoduodenal, Jejunal/Ileal, Ileocolic, Right Colic, Middle Colic
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the specific branch of the Superior Mesenteric Artery (SMA) supplying the cecum and ascending colon, differentiating it from other SMA branches and the Inferior Mesenteric Artery (IMA) branches. (识别上肠系膜动脉 (SMA) 的分支，该分支供应盲肠和升结肠，并将其与其他 SMA 分支和下肠系膜动脉 (IMA) 分支区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral vascular disease presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe abdominal pain, nausea, and vomiting. He reports having black, tarry stools for the past 2 days. Physical examination reveals abdominal distension, tenderness to palpation in the right lower quadrant, and guarding. His vital signs show tachycardia (110 bpm) and hypotension (90/60 mmHg). An emergent CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis with intravenous contrast reveals a large area of bowel wall thickening and pneumatosis intestinalis involving the cecum and ascending colon, consistent with ischemic colitis. There is also evidence of mesenteric edema and a paucity of contrast enhancement within the vessels supplying this region. The CT angiogram specifically shows occlusion of a major branch of the SMA distal to the origin of the inferior pancreaticoduodenal artery, but proximal to the ileocolic artery. Which of the following arteries is most likely occluded?

(A) Inferior Pancreaticoduodenal Artery
(B) Jejunal Artery
(C) Ileocolic Artery
(D) Right Colic Artery
(E) Middle Colic Artery

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 68-year-old male, poorly controlled diabetes, peripheral vascular disease, acute severe abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, melena (black tarry stools), abdominal distension, RLQ tenderness/guarding, tachycardia, hypotension. This suggests acute mesenteric ischemia.
- **CT Findings:** Bowel wall thickening, pneumatosis intestinalis (air in bowel wall), mesenteric edema, paucity of contrast enhancement in the cecum and ascending colon. This confirms ischemic colitis affecting the right colon.
- **CT Angiogram:** Occlusion of a major SMA branch distal to the inferior pancreaticoduodenal artery and proximal to the ileocolic artery. This is the crucial anatomical clue.
- **Question Stem:** Asks to identify the specific SMA branch occluded based on its anatomical position relative to other branches and the affected bowel segment (cecum and ascending colon).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Superior Mesenteric Artery (SMA) Branches:** The SMA arises from the abdominal aorta just below the celiac trunk. Its major branches, in proximal to distal order, are:
    1.  **Inferior Pancreaticoduodenal Artery:** Supplies the head of the pancreas and duodenum.
    2.  **Jejunal Artery:** Supplies the jejunum.
    3.  **Ileal Artery:** Supplies the ileum.
    4.  **Ileocolic Artery:** Supplies the terminal ileum, cecum, appendix, and proximal ascending colon.
    5.  **Right Colic Artery:** Supplies the ascending colon.
    6.  **Middle Colic Artery:** Supplies the transverse colon.
- **Anatomical Relationships:** The ileocolic artery arises from the SMA and travels inferiorly and to the right, passing behind the ileum to reach the cecum. The right colic artery typically arises from the SMA just distal to the ileocolic artery (though variations exist, it can arise from the ileocolic or even the middle colic). The middle colic artery arises from the SMA and supplies the transverse colon. The inferior pancreaticoduodenal artery is one of the most proximal branches.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The CT angiogram localizes the occlusion distal to the inferior pancreaticoduodenal artery (ruling out A) and proximal to the ileocolic artery (ruling out C). The affected area is the cecum and ascending colon. The ileocolic artery supplies the cecum and proximal ascending colon. The right colic artery supplies the rest of the ascending colon. Given the occlusion is *proximal* to the ileocolic artery, the right colic artery is the next branch supplying the affected region (ascending colon). The jejunal artery supplies the jejunum, which is not affected. Therefore, the occlusion must involve the right colic artery. Ischemia of the cecum and ascending colon is a classic presentation of SMA branch occlusion, often involving the right colic artery.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Inferior Pancreaticoduodenal Artery:** This is a proximal branch of the SMA, supplying the head of the pancreas and duodenum. Occlusion would cause pancreatic or duodenal ischemia, not cecal/ascending colonic ischemia. The vignette explicitly states the occlusion is *distal* to this artery. Incorrect.
- (B) **Jejunal Artery:** This branch supplies the jejunum. The clinical presentation and CT findings involve the cecum and ascending colon, not the jejunum. Incorrect.
- (C) **Ileocolic Artery:** This artery supplies the cecum, appendix, and proximal ascending colon. The vignette states the occlusion is *proximal* to the ileocolic artery. Therefore, the ileocolic artery itself cannot be the occluded vessel. Incorrect.
- (E) **Middle Colic Artery:** This artery supplies the transverse colon. While it's a branch of the SMA, the affected region in this case is the cecum and ascending colon, not the transverse colon. The occlusion is also described as being proximal to the ileocolic artery, placing the middle colic artery even further distally. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Artery                     | Origin from SMA        | Supply Area                                  | Location Relative to Occlusion | Correct? |
| :-------------------------- | :--------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :------- |
| Inferior Pancreaticoduodenal | Proximal SMA           | Head of Pancreas, Duodenum                   | Proximal to Occlusion          | No       |
| Jejunal                     | Mid SMA                | Jejunum                                      | Distal to Affected Area        | No       |
| Ileocolic                   | Mid-Distal SMA         | Cecum, Appendix, Proximal Ascending Colon     | Distal to Occlusion            | No       |
| **Right Colic**             | **Mid-Distal SMA**     | **Ascending Colon**                          | **Site of Occlusion**          | **Yes**  |
| Middle Colic                | Distal SMA             | Transverse Colon                              | Distal to Occlusion            | No       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The occlusion of the right colic artery, a branch of the SMA located distal to the ileocolic artery and proximal to the middle colic artery, classically causes ischemia of the ascending colon. Differentiating SMA branches based on their origin sequence and supply territory is crucial for diagnosing mesenteric ischemia. Key differentials include occlusion of the IMA branches (left colic, sigmoid arteries) affecting the left colon, or occlusion of the SMA trunk itself (more proximal, affecting all distal branches).

---

<a id='question-065'></a>

### Question 065: SMA Syndrome: Compression of 3rd Part of Duodenum between SMA and Aorta in Rapid Weight Loss
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the anatomical relationship between the superior mesenteric artery (SMA), the aorta, and the third part of the duodenum, specifically how this relationship can lead to compression (SMA syndrome) in the context of rapid weight loss and cachexia. 考察横结肠系膜上动脉 (SMA)、主动脉和十二指肠第三部分之间的解剖关系，以及这种关系在快速减肥和恶液质背景下如何导致压迫 (SMA 综合征)。

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 22-year-old female college student presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, intermittent abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, and early satiety for the past 3 months. She reports a history of anorexia nervosa and has lost 30 kg (66 lbs) in the last 6 months. Her physical examination reveals significant cachexia, with prominent clavicles and loss of subcutaneous fat. Abdominal examination shows mild epigastric tenderness but no guarding or rebound. Laboratory tests show electrolyte abnormalities consistent with malnutrition. An abdominal CT scan reveals a markedly narrowed angle between the superior mesenteric artery (SMA) and the aorta, with compression of the third part of the duodenum between these two structures. The duodenum proximal to the compression is dilated.

Which of the following anatomical structures is most likely compressed in this patient's condition?

(A) The common bile duct
(B) The portal vein
(C) The inferior mesenteric artery
(D) The third part of the duodenum
(E) The right renal artery

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** Young female with anorexia nervosa and significant weight loss (cachexia). This points towards a condition exacerbated by loss of retroperitoneal fat.
- **Symptoms:** Intermittent abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, early satiety. These are classic symptoms of duodenal obstruction.
- **Physical Exam:** Cachexia, epigastric tenderness. Consistent with malnutrition and potential upper GI pathology.
- **Imaging (CT Scan):** "Markedly narrowed angle between the superior mesenteric artery (SMA) and the aorta, with compression of the third part of the duodenum between these two structures. The duodenum proximal to the compression is dilated." This is the definitive finding for SMA syndrome. The question asks what structure is compressed.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **SMA Syndrome:** Also known as Wilkie's syndrome, is a rare condition where the third part of the duodenum is compressed between the SMA and the aorta.
- **Normal Anatomy:** The SMA arises from the aorta at the level of L1, typically at an angle of 35-55 degrees relative to the aorta. The third part of the duodenum passes horizontally to the left, inferior to the SMA and anterior to the aorta. The normal distance between the SMA and the aorta is maintained by the retroperitoneal fat pad (of Landzert).
- **Pathophysiology:** In SMA syndrome, the angle between the SMA and the aorta decreases (often <25 degrees) due to loss of the retroperitoneal fat pad, typically secondary to rapid weight loss, prolonged bed rest, or certain malignancies. This reduced angle brings the SMA and aorta closer together, compressing the duodenum.
- **Duodenum Anatomy:** The duodenum is divided into four parts. The third part (horizontal portion) runs horizontally across the midline, anterior to the inferior vena cava (IVC) and aorta, and inferior to the SMA. It is the most mobile part and is susceptible to compression.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's history of anorexia nervosa and rapid weight loss directly explains the loss of retroperitoneal fat, leading to the narrowed SMA-aorta angle and subsequent compression of the third part of the duodenum, causing the observed symptoms of partial duodenal obstruction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **The common bile duct:** The common bile duct typically passes posterior to the first part of the duodenum and then through the head of the pancreas to join the main pancreatic duct before entering the duodenum at the ampulla of Vater. It is not located between the SMA and the aorta and is therefore not compressed in SMA syndrome.
- (B) **The portal vein:** The portal vein is formed posterior to the neck of the pancreas by the confluence of the superior mesenteric vein and the splenic vein. It ascends within the hepatoduodenal ligament, posterior to the first part of the duodenum, and enters the liver. It is not located between the SMA and the aorta.
- (C) **The inferior mesenteric artery (IMA):** The IMA arises from the aorta at the level of L3, inferior to the origin of the SMA. It supplies the distal transverse colon, descending colon, sigmoid colon, and rectum. It is located inferior to the area of compression in SMA syndrome.
- (E) **The right renal artery:** The right renal artery arises directly from the abdominal aorta, just inferior to the SMA, at the level of L1/L2. It passes posterior to the IVC and renal vein to reach the right kidney. While it is in the vicinity, it is not located between the SMA and the aorta and is not compressed in SMA syndrome.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比)

| Structure                 | Location Relative to SMA/Aorta | Relationship to Duodenum | Compressed in SMA Syndrome? |
| :------------------------ | :----------------------------- | :----------------------- | :-------------------------- |
| **Third Part of Duodenum** | Between SMA and Aorta          | Passes horizontally below SMA, anterior to Aorta | **Yes**                     |
| Common Bile Duct          | Posterior to 1st Duodenal Part | Enters duodenum at head | No                         |
| Portal Vein               | Posterior to 1st Duodenal Part | Ascends in hepatoduodenal ligament | No                         |
| Inferior Mesenteric Artery | Arises from Aorta (L3)        | Supplies distal colon    | No                         |
| Right Renal Artery        | Arises from Aorta (L1/L2)      | Passes posterior to IVC  | No                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
SMA syndrome is caused by compression of the third part of the duodenum between the SMA and the aorta, typically due to loss of retroperitoneal fat. Key differentials for duodenal obstruction include peptic ulcer disease, malignancy, and adhesions. Remember the specific anatomical location of the third part of the duodenum relative to the SMA and aorta.

<a id='question-066'></a>

### Question 066: Inferior Mesenteric Artery (IMA) Origin and Branches
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The origin of the Inferior Mesenteric Artery (IMA) from the abdominal aorta, typically at the level of the L3 vertebra, and its major branches supplying the distal colon and rectum (left colic, sigmoid, and superior rectal arteries). (下肠系膜动脉 (IMA) 起源于腹主动脉，通常在 L3 椎骨水平，其主要分支为左结肠动脉、乙状结肠动脉和上直肠动脉。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia undergoes an elective open sigmoid colectomy for diverticular disease. During the procedure, the surgeon identifies the inferior mesenteric artery (IMA) arising from the anterior aspect of the abdominal aorta. The IMA is carefully ligated and divided proximal to its bifurcation. The surgeon then identifies the first major branch arising inferiorly from the IMA, which courses laterally to supply the descending colon and the splenic flexure. Following this, the surgeon identifies the next branch, which courses inferiorly and to the left to supply the sigmoid colon. Finally, the surgeon identifies the terminal branch, which descends into the pelvis to supply the upper rectum. Postoperatively, the patient develops symptoms suggestive of ischemic colitis involving the distal descending colon and proximal sigmoid colon. Reviewing intraoperative images, the surgeon notes that the first branch identified after the IMA origin was inadvertently ligated along with the IMA itself.

Which of the following arteries was most likely ligated along with the IMA, leading to the patient's postoperative ischemic colitis?

(A) Superior Rectal Artery
(B) Middle Rectal Artery
(C) Left Colic Artery
(D) Sigmoid Arteries
(E) Ileocolic Artery

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male undergoing sigmoid colectomy. This sets the context for abdominal surgery involving the distal colon.
- **Procedure:** Open sigmoid colectomy, involving ligation and division of the IMA.
- **Anatomical Landmark:** IMA origin from the abdominal aorta at L3 level (implied by standard anatomy).
- **Branch Identification Sequence:** The surgeon identifies branches in a specific order:
    1.  First branch (lateral to descending colon/splenic flexure)
    2.  Second branch (inferior and left to sigmoid colon)
    3.  Terminal branch (descends into pelvis for upper rectum)
- **Surgical Error:** The *first* branch identified was ligated along with the IMA.
- **Postoperative Complication:** Ischemic colitis affecting the distal descending colon and proximal sigmoid colon.
- **Question:** Identify the artery ligated along with the IMA that supplies this affected region.

The key clue is the sequence of branch identification and the location of the resulting ischemia. The first branch identified after the IMA origin, supplying the distal descending colon and splenic flexure, is the Left Colic Artery. Ligation of this artery explains the ischemia in the distal descending colon and proximal sigmoid colon.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **IMA Origin:** The Inferior Mesenteric Artery (IMA) typically arises from the anterior surface of the abdominal aorta at the level of the L3 vertebra, just below the origin of the superior mesenteric artery (SMA) at L1/L2.
- **IMA Branches:** The IMA has three main branches:
    1.  **Left Colic Artery:** The first and largest branch. It arises from the IMA near its origin and runs laterally to supply the distal third of the transverse colon, the splenic flexure, and the descending colon down to the sigmoid colon. (Kaplan, FA 2025)
    2.  **Sigmoid Arteries:** These are typically 2-4 small branches arising from the IMA below the left colic artery. They supply the sigmoid colon. (Kaplan, FA 2025)
    3.  **Superior Rectal Artery:** The terminal branch of the IMA. It descends into the pelvis, passes through the mesorectum, and supplies the upper part of the rectum. (Kaplan, FA 2025)
- **Clinical Correlation:** Ligation of the IMA during surgery (e.g., sigmoid colectomy) can compromise blood supply to the distal colon and rectum. Ligation of the Left Colic Artery specifically would lead to ischemia of the distal descending colon and splenic flexure, matching the patient's symptoms. The Sigmoid Arteries supply the sigmoid colon, and the Superior Rectal Artery supplies the upper rectum. The Middle Rectal Artery (from the internal iliac) and Inferior Rectal Artery (from the internal pudendal) supply the middle and lower rectum, respectively. The Ileocolic Artery is a branch of the SMA supplying the cecum, appendix, and proximal ascending colon.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Superior Rectal Artery:** This is the terminal branch of the IMA, supplying the upper rectum. Ligation would cause ischemia of the upper rectum, not the distal descending/proximal sigmoid colon. The surgeon identified this as the *terminal* branch, not the first one.
- (B) **Middle Rectal Artery:** This artery arises from the internal iliac artery, not the IMA. It supplies the middle part of the rectum. It is not a branch of the IMA and therefore not ligated during IMA ligation.
- (D) **Sigmoid Arteries:** These arteries arise *after* the Left Colic Artery from the IMA and supply the sigmoid colon. While the patient has ischemia in the proximal sigmoid colon, the primary affected area described (distal descending colon) is supplied by the Left Colic Artery. The vignette states the *first* branch was ligated.
- (E) **Ileocolic Artery:** This is a major branch of the Superior Mesenteric Artery (SMA), supplying the cecum, appendix, and ascending colon. It is not related to the IMA or the surgical field described.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Left Colic Artery (Correct Answer) | Sigmoid Arteries | Superior Rectal Artery | Middle Rectal Artery | Ileocolic Artery |
| :------------------ | :--------------------------------- | :--------------- | :--------------------- | :------------------- | :--------------- |
| **Origin**          | IMA                                | IMA              | IMA                   | Internal Iliac       | SMA              |
| **Level of Origin** | Near IMA origin (L3)               | Below Left Colic | Terminal IMA branch   | Pelvis               | L1/L2            |
| **Supply Area**     | Distal Descending Colon, Splenic Flexure | Sigmoid Colon    | Upper Rectum          | Middle Rectum       | Cecum, Ascending Colon |
| **Relation to IMA** | First major branch                 | Intermediate    | Terminal branch       | Not IMA branch      | Not IMA branch   |
| **Clinical Relevance**| Ligation -> Distal Descending/Splenic Flexure Ischemia | Ligation -> Sigmoid Ischemia | Ligation -> Upper Rectal Ischemia | N/A              | N/A              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The Inferior Mesenteric Artery (IMA) arises at L3 and gives off three main branches in sequence: Left Colic, Sigmoid, and Superior Rectal arteries. Knowing the order and distribution of these branches is crucial for understanding the consequences of IMA ligation during surgery. Remember the Left Colic Artery supplies the distal descending colon and splenic flexure. Key differentials include the branches of the SMA (Ileocolic, Right Colic, Middle Colic) and branches of the internal iliac artery (Middle and Inferior Rectal).

<a id='question-067'></a>

### Question 067: Vascular Supply of the Colon
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The marginal artery of Drummond (also known as the arterial arcade of Drummond) is a continuous arterial arcade running along the inner border of the colon, formed by anastomoses between branches of the superior mesenteric artery (SMA) and inferior mesenteric artery (IMA). Understanding its formation, location, and clinical significance is crucial. (马氏肠边动脉/德鲁蒙德动脉弧是沿结肠内缘连续的动脉弧，由上肠系膜动脉(SMA)和下肠系膜动脉(IMA)的支脉之间吻合而成。理解其形成、位置和临床意义至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe abdominal pain, nausea, and vomiting. He reports having black, tarry stools for the past 24 hours. Physical examination reveals a distended abdomen with diffuse tenderness, guarding, and rebound tenderness, most pronounced in the left lower quadrant. Vital signs show tachycardia (110 bpm) and hypotension (90/60 mmHg). An emergent CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis with intravenous contrast reveals signs of bowel ischemia, specifically involving a segment of the sigmoid colon. The CT angiogram demonstrates occlusion of the left colic artery, with evidence of collateral circulation via a continuous arterial arcade along the mesenteric border of the affected colon segment. This arcade appears to be compromised, leading to the observed ischemia. Which of the following anatomical structures is primarily responsible for the collateral circulation observed in this patient?

(A) Ileocolic artery branches supplying the cecum and ascending colon.
(B) Superior mesenteric artery branches supplying the proximal transverse colon.
(C) Inferior mesenteric artery branches supplying the distal transverse colon and descending colon.
(D) Marginal artery of Drummond connecting branches of the superior mesenteric artery and inferior mesenteric artery along the inner border of the colon.
(E) Arc of Riolan connecting the superior mesenteric artery and celiac trunk.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 68-year-old male with risk factors (HTN, hyperlipidemia) presenting with acute abdominal pain, melena, distension, tenderness, guarding, rebound (peritonitis), tachycardia, hypotension (shock). This suggests acute mesenteric ischemia.
- **Location:** Symptoms and tenderness are most pronounced in the left lower quadrant, implicating the sigmoid colon or descending colon.
- **Imaging Findings:** CT angiogram shows occlusion of the left colic artery (a branch of the IMA) and bowel ischemia in the sigmoid colon.
- **Collateral Circulation:** The CT angiogram specifically mentions a "continuous arterial arcade along the mesenteric border of the affected colon segment" providing collateral circulation, which is compromised.
- **Question Stem:** Asks to identify the anatomical structure responsible for this specific collateral circulation pattern.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Marginal Artery of Drummond (Arcade of Drummond):** This is a crucial anatomical structure. It is a continuous arterial arcade that runs along the *inner* border (mesenteric border) of the colon, from the distal transverse colon to the upper rectum.
- **Formation:** It is formed by anastomoses between the terminal branches of the superior mesenteric artery (SMA) and the inferior mesenteric artery (IMA). Specifically, it connects the ileocolic artery (from SMA) to the left colic artery (from IMA) via the right colic artery and middle colic artery (from SMA) and the sigmoid arteries (from IMA).
- **Clinical Significance:** It provides collateral circulation between the SMA and IMA territories. If one major artery (like the left colic artery in this case) is occluded, the marginal artery can potentially supply blood to the affected segment of the colon, preventing or delaying ischemia. However, if the occlusion is proximal or severe, or if the marginal artery itself is compromised (e.g., by atherosclerosis), ischemia can still occur. The vignette describes this scenario where the left colic artery is occluded, and the marginal artery is the source of collateral flow, albeit compromised.
- **Location:** Key point - it runs along the *inner* border of the colon, within the mesentery.
- **Contrast with other options:**
    - (A) Ileocolic artery supplies the cecum/ascending colon (SMA territory). While it contributes to the marginal artery, it's not the *entire* arcade responsible for collateral flow along the *entire* inner border, especially in the left colon segment affected here.
    - (B) SMA branches supply the proximal transverse colon. Similar to (A), they contribute but aren't the complete structure described.
    - (C) IMA branches supply the distal transverse, descending, and sigmoid colon. They also contribute to the marginal artery, but again, the *arcade itself* is the continuous structure.
    - (E) Arc of Riolan connects the SMA and celiac trunk, typically located posterior to the pancreas, supplying the stomach, spleen, and pancreas. It is not related to the colon's blood supply.

##### 3. Embryological Basis (胚胎发生基础)
- The formation of the marginal artery reflects the sequential development of the midgut and hindgut. The midgut (supplied by SMA) and hindgut (supplied by IMA) initially have separate arterial supplies. The marginal artery represents the persistent anastomotic connections between these two systems, which are crucial for collateral circulation in the adult.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The ileocolic artery is a branch of the SMA supplying the cecum, appendix, and terminal ileum. It contributes to the marginal artery, but it is not the continuous arcade itself, nor is it the primary source of collateral flow to the *left* colon segment affected in this case. Test-takers might confuse it because it's part of the SMA territory and contributes to the arcade.
- (B): Superior mesenteric artery branches supply the proximal transverse colon. While the middle colic artery (an SMA branch) contributes to the marginal artery, this option is too general and doesn't describe the specific continuous arcade responsible for collateral flow along the entire inner border, especially in the affected left colon.
- (C): Inferior mesenteric artery branches supply the distal transverse colon, descending colon, and sigmoid colon. The left colic artery (IMA branch) is occluded in the vignette, and sigmoid arteries (IMA branches) contribute to the arcade. However, this option describes the *source* arteries rather than the *anastomotic arcade* itself, which is the structure providing the collateral circulation. Test-takers might choose this because the affected segment is supplied by the IMA.
- (E): The arc of Riolan (or pancreatic arcade) is an anastomosis between the celiac trunk and the superior mesenteric artery, located behind the neck of the pancreas. It supplies the stomach, spleen, and pancreas. It has no direct relationship to the blood supply of the colon. This is a common distractor testing knowledge of major abdominal arterial arcades.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                                     | Blood Supply Origin                                  | Function                                                                 | Relevance to Vignette                                                                                                |
| :---------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Marginal Artery of Drummond** | **Inner border (mesenteric border) of colon** | **Anastomoses between SMA & IMA branches**           | **Collateral circulation between SMA & IMA territories**                 | **Directly responsible for the described collateral circulation in the occluded left colic artery territory.**          |
| Ileocolic Artery              | Mesentery of cecum, appendix, terminal ileum | Superior Mesenteric Artery (SMA)                    | Supplies cecum, appendix, terminal ileum                                | Contributes to the marginal artery, but not the continuous arcade itself; supplies a different segment of the colon. |
| Left Colic Artery             | Mesentery of descending/sigmoid colon         | Inferior Mesenteric Artery (IMA)                    | Supplies descending/sigmoid colon                                       | Occluded in the vignette, leading to ischemia despite collateral flow.                                                |
| Arc of Riolan                 | Behind pancreas                              | Anastomoses between Celiac Trunk & SMA               | Supplies stomach, spleen, pancreas                                      | Incorrect location and function; unrelated to colon blood supply.                                                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The marginal artery of Drummond is a critical collateral pathway between the SMA and IMA territories along the inner border of the colon. Occlusion of a major branch (like the left colic artery) can lead to ischemia, but the marginal artery may provide sufficient collateral flow to prevent infarction, although it may be compromised by disease. Differentiate the marginal artery from other arcades like the Arc of Riolan and understand the specific territories supplied by SMA and IMA branches. Key differentials include the specific branches of SMA (ileocolic, right colic, middle colic) and IMA (left colic, sigmoid arteries) and their contribution to the marginal artery versus being the arcade itself.
